"Soong Chu-yu! Chang Chau-hsiung! Soong Chu-yu! Chang Chau-hsiung!"

The chants squeezed from the depths of throats sounded less like slogans and more like drowning souls clinging to a single piece of driftwood. Stage lights threw his shadow long and thin against the massive billboard behind him. Beside the gold-lettered slogan "The People Are the Masters," he and Chang Chau-hsiung smiled side by side in light jackets, unbuttoned at the collar, as if fresh off a country tour.

He turned to smile at Chen Wan-sui below the stage. Wearing her lotus-pink jacket, her hands were cupped around her mouth as she joined the chanting, her eyes slightly red with excitement. His son, Soong Chen-yuan, stood beside her in black-rimmed glasses, offering a timid smile like a university student pulling an all-nighter.

Raising his microphone, the wind rushed past, snapping the party banners. He caught the scent of sweat, fried chicken cutlets, and the distinct heat of earth and plastic bags from a ten-thousand-strong rally. "My fellow countrymen—what Chu-yu says to you today is no mere campaign language, but what I have witnessed firsthand traveling across three hundred and nineteen townships over the past twenty years!"

Light placards reading "Governor Soong Save Taiwan" rose from the crowd, featuring hand-painted portraits of him cradling infants, ink blurred by rain and retraced again.

"Some say my polls are falling," he spoke candidly, drawing a wave of jeers from the crowd. "Chu-yu does not lie—they have fallen. But who conducted those polls? The China Times? The United Daily News? Polls bought with campaign ad money issued by the Kuomintang! We Taiwanese have an old saying: 'A bad bamboo stalk yields good shoots.' Those media bosses want to be bad bamboo, but I, James Soong, am that good shoot!"

Wan-shui bent over laughing, touching her son's arm while Chen-yuan adjusted his glasses, the tips of his ears flushed red.

Lying down that night, images flooded his mind. The flame tree outside the provincial government window, blooming red in June—he never let workers sweep the fallen petals, saying "Leave a little red for decoration." He remembered visiting the mountains of Nantou in 1998 after a landslide washed out the access road. He stepped out of his car and walked three kilometers on foot while his young accompanying section chief leaned against bamboo stalks, gasping for air, while Soong barely lost his breath. In those days his stride was quick, leaping across ditches and reaching back to pull those behind him. In those days, he could still pull others.

He remembered driving into Puli after the 921 earthquake without bodyguards or motorcades, taking only his driver and two crates of bottled water, navigating four hours of detours to get in. For the next two months he virtually lived in the disaster zone, sleeping on elementary school desks under thin cotton blankets. When an aftershock struck, he rolled off the desk, striking his back against the podium and leaving a palm-sized bruise, yet inspected ten relief centers as usual the following morning. Local village chiefs were stunned: "Chairman Soong, why are you still here? We thought you returned to Taipei." He said nothing, thinking to himself:Return to Taipei? Is there still a place for me there?* But in the disaster zone, nobody sidelined him. Every outstretched hand held a genuine cup of hot tea, and every "Thank you for your hard work, Chairman Soong" was heartfelt.

He remembered the veterans in the retirement homes. Old Zhao, ninety-three, a native of Henan who arrived in 1949, stood at attention and saluted "Greetings, Deputy Secretary-General!" every time he visited. He had shouted that title since Soong actually served as Deputy Secretary-General, continuing long after Soong stepped down as Governor. Soong spent a decade trying to correct him before realizing Old Zhao wasn't confused—he was seeking a gentle correction to keep Soong standing beside his bed for five minutes longer.

Turning over in bed, Wan-shui patted his back half-asleep: "Go to sleep, you have to vote early tomorrow." He held her hand, her finger bones slender as a small bird. He had held this hand for over thirty years, from a small apartment in Maryland to the long corridors of the provincial government. When he was completing his doctorate at Georgetown University and Wan-shui worked in the library, their monthly living budget was under four hundred dollars. On weekends they bought discounted chicken legs, brewing the bones into broth to drink for three days. He suddenly felt that if he lost tomorrow, the person he would fail most was not himself, but her. Yet the next second he pushed the thought down—I won't lose. How could a man who walked three hundred and nineteen townships lose?

On election day, the sunlight was brilliant. He and Wan-shui walked to the elementary school near their home amid a barrage of camera shutters. He wore an old dark blue suit; Wan-shui wore a light gray silk scarf. Before entering the polling station, she released his arm and smiled at him—a smile that meant not "Good luck," but "No matter what happens, I am here." Standing behind the curtain, he picked up the stamp and pressed it beside his name. The ink was wet, bleeding a small red halo around the edges. Folding the ballot, he dropped it into the box, making a faint clack* as it reached the bottom.

That evening, Wan-shui cooked a pot of braised pork, simmering it until the meat yielded to chopsticks. He remarked that the soy sauce proportion was just right today.

That night, what he expected never arrived. Returns trickled in from the south first, showing him ahead; in central Taiwan, he was neck and neck with Lien Chan; then the north opened—Taipei, New Taipei, Keelung—and votes rolled back from beneath his feet like a receding tide. The Yan-Ching scandal, opinion polls, strategic voting—subsequent news reports offered an entire array of explanations, telling him the signs were clear from the start. But sitting on a plastic chair in the holding room before a cold cup of tea, only one thought occupied his mind: So the hands shaken at temple plazas, the faces whose names I could call out, the voices promising 'I will vote for you this time'—added together, were still not enough. It wasn't that his memory was too good; it was that he treated memories as debts, assuming everyone owed the same sum.

Leaving headquarters past midnight, orange campaign posters lay crushed in the hallway, his face smeared by shoe soles. Picking up a scrap to smooth it out, the wet paper tore at his touch. He placed the fragment in his pocket. Wan-shui waited in the car. The rain had stopped, and water droplets on the windshield glowed like molten glass under streetlights. He sat inside without starting the engine, resting his hands on the steering wheel while staring down the dark alley. Only after a long silence did he speak: "I didn't cry." Wan-shui replied: "I know." "It's not that I didn't want to cry." "I know."

after 2012 election,Six months later in the heat of summer, Wan-shui passed away.

By 2016 and 2020, when he stood atop campaign trucks once more, only the wind remained at his side.

Removing his hands from the steering wheel, his knuckles felt stiff. The steering wheel vanished. Beneath his palms lay a cold plastic folding table. A laptop displayed the live EBC news broadcast split into three columns: Tsai Ing-wen, Han Kuo-yu, James Soong. Numbers kept ticking. The air carried a dry, bitter scent—not mold, but the dead silence unique to a room unbreathed in for too long.

A rented business center conference room, billed by the hour. Low ceilings, a single flickering fluorescent light casting pale shadows over seven or eight seated staffers in orange vests scrolling on smartphones. Nobody cried, nobody laughed; it wasn't even anxiety, resembling students waiting for final exam grades—knowing they failed, yet hoping for a miracle.

It was January 11, 2020, 4:31 PM. He was seventy-seven years old.

Gazing at the orange bar on the screen, stumpy like an underdeveloped child shrinking timidly at the bottom, the scale nearly touching zero. But then, it moved. At first he thought his eyes were playing tricks—the slender orange bar shot upward in an unreasonable vertical surge, like a server error curve. Exclamations broke out in the adjacent room as the news anchor's voice cracked: "...a major anomaly appears on the Central Election Commission website as PFP candidate James Soong's vote tally surges dramatically within two minutes..."

Footsteps echoed in the hallway, knocking over a water bottle that spilled with a faint trickle like quiet weeping. He remained seated, slowly lowering his hands to his knees. The lining of his suit sleeve bore a small worn hole; touching it, he confirmed it was real.

He knew what those numbers represented now: ${FINAL_VOTE_PERCENT.toFixed(1)}%. A victory delayed by twenty years, arriving so late that no one remained to raise their hands alongside him. He recalled an old reporter asking him days after the 2000 election what he planned to do next, to which he blurted out: "I will keep walking, because many in Taiwan have not reached the end." Back then he hadn't looked at the camera, but out the window, as if "walking" was no metaphor.

He really did keep walking. Partnering with Lien Chan in 2004, standing in the sunset of that election as a symbol—not of victory, but of tolerance. In 2012 Wan-shui fought the final campaign by his side. In 2016, and 2020, he returned every time, the crowds shrinking slightly each round like reprinted old photographs losing clarity until only outlines remained.

But crowd size was never the point. The point was that he could not stop. Not out of obsession with power, but because he believed Taiwan required a Third Way between blue and green. He was not the first to travel this road; long ago others had labored between two giant parties—Lin Yang-Kang And Chen Li-an, and forgotten names before him. They did not succeed in their lifetimes, and Soong was neither the first nor the last. The orange flag of the PFP was merely a marker along this road. As to whether he was the most influential—he had no answer. That belonged to future wisdom. Perhaps the current Mayor of Taipei stood at a new intersection holding another possibility. The story of a third force was never an individual biography, but an unfinished catalog where every chapter ended with "To be continued" and began with a new name.

Standing up, he walked to the window and parted the blinds. No crowds below, no cheers, no news trucks. An old lottery vendor was packing his stall, placing scratch cards into plastic boxes and pushing his cart down the alley, wheels clacking over drainage covers in a limping three-beat rhythm—slow, yet moving forward.

Releasing the blinds, he turned back to the seven or eight pairs of eyes—filled with panic, confusion, and a burgeoning joy they hardly dared trust. But in none of those eyes did he find that specific heat—the fire that pulsed even on defeat nights in 1998 and 2000. Today those eyes viewed him like an old tortoise behind glass—there was respect, but respect for the abstract concept of longevity rather than the creature itself. Nobody cared what the tortoise thought behind the glass.

${currentMateName} handed over a printed tally sheet, the paper still warm. Her voice was soft, like an apology to a fragile object: "Chairman... would you like to take a look?"

Taking the paper, he folded it thrice and slipped it into his inner suit pocket with deliberate slowness. Taking a sip of cold tea from his cup, it tasted bitterly astringent—the bitterness of a tea bag steeped over an hour. He didn't frown. He recalled Wan-shui teasing him for hanging a "Heaven Rewards Diligence" calligraphy scroll for twenty years without changing it; he had considered replacing it with "Full Moon and Flourishing Flowers" before tearing it down a day later. Now he understood what that phrase meant—it meant having someone beside you complaining about your outdated handwriting, then grinding fresh ink for you anyway.

Setting the cup down, he spoke softly: "Is there hot water? I'd like to brew a fresh cup."

Nobody answered.

He didn't ask again. Slipping a finger into the worn hole of his sleeve, he sat down quietly, waiting for a cup of hot tea that didn't exist, waiting for a person who would never return to bring it to him. Outside the window, the lottery vendor had vanished down the alleyway like a drop of ink dissolving in a bowl of water, silent and trace-free. Inside the conference room, fluorescent lights flickered on, stretching everyone into fluctuating shadows—passengers in a fading photograph, waiting for the page to turn.

January 11th. 4:01 PM.

An old radio tucked in the corner of James Soong's campaign headquarters suddenly let out a piercing screech—like a cat having its tail stepped on and a broken police siren stuck on the same channel simultaneously. It crackled with static for a moment before broadcasting a brief snippet of Taiwanese news: "...Tsai Ing-wen in Pingtung..." then cut off, devolving into pure white noise, as if someone had dumped an entire bag of salt onto the microphone.

Nobody went to turn it off. Young volunteers gathered around a laptop, constantly refreshing the returns page where numbers had just begun to tick.

James Soong stood right beside that old radio, holding a cup of hot black tea bought from the bento shop next door. Bending over, he patted the top cover of the radio twice with his palm, neither too hard nor too light. The static paused for a second, then returned. A senior party secretary standing closest to him failed to catch the sentence he mumbled. Stepping closer, he asked, "What did you say, Chairman?"

"I said," Soong straightened his back, lifting the tea bag to drip against the rim of his cup, "this radio has run five presidential elections with us. It's time for it to go."

He didn't explain whether "time to go" referred to the radio, to himself, or to the entire People First Party. The party secretary stood frozen in place. Soong patted his shoulder and smiled: "Just joking. It's only four o'clock. The cable pundits on television haven't even started talking nonsense yet, and your face has already fallen. Relax."

When he smiled, three layers of wrinkles folded around the corners of his eyes, carrying not exhaustion, but something approaching relief. The people around him weren't quite used to this relief. After all, this was James Soong's fifth presidential campaign, and quite possibly his last. All the final blackout polls had pinned him in single-digit territory, like a pushpin stuck into a bulletin board—it wouldn't fall, but nobody would give it a second look either.

At 4:30 PM, official Central Election Commission counts began to accelerate.

Soong sat on a folding chair in the corner of headquarters. The chair was a relic from the 2012 campaign, its back still bearing a faded "Soong-Lin" sticker. Lin was Lin Ruey-shiung, a name rarely mentioned anymore. He sat on the very edge of the chair, knees not fully bent, as if ready to stand up and walk somewhere at any moment. His posture was identical to the role he had played in Taiwan's political arena for the past twenty years: present on stage, but positioned as if ready to leave at a moment's notice.

A reporter shoved a microphone toward him, asking how he viewed the overall battle unfolding across the island. Soong glanced at the television screen where the blue and green trend lines were rapidly pulling apart.

"Mayor Han served as mayor in Kaohsiung," Soong said in a flat tone, as if discussing a minor traffic accident at someone else's alleyway. "The people of Kaohsiung gave him his annual performance review with their ballots." He used no sharp words, nor did he feign regret. He knew the camera was trained on him, seeking to capture an old politician throwing stones at another. But he was long past that stage. He cared about only one thing now.

That one thing was a sequence of numbers.

The PFP's party-list vote percentage, tied directly to his presidential vote percentage. Under election law, if a presidential candidate's vote share exceeds five percent, that party holds the right to directly nominate a presidential ticket next time without gathering public signatures. There was also a state subsidy payout. The sum wasn't astronomical, but it was enough to keep a party running on two offices and three full-time staffers alive for another four years.

Four point nine percent versus five percent was a difference of zero to one in mathematics, and the exact moment an umbilical cord was severed in politics.

Soong called this the "umbilical cord line". Around six in the evening, he mentioned the term to the widow of his old friend, former legislator Liu Wen-hsiung, who had come to headquarters to help out. She froze for a moment, then shook her head, unsure whether to smile or sigh.

On the giant TV wall, news channels broadcast live coverage from both major party campaign headquarters. Across the sea, the storm over Hong Kong had raged for over half a year, blowing the pan-blue base's remaining confidence paper-thin. Compounded by the "runaway mayor" moniker stuck like chewing gum to his shoes through the winter, the man who once stirred a hurricane in Kaohsiung was blown hollow like an empty flagpole. Beside Soong stood running mate Sandra Yu. Wearing a dark blue blazer, she conversed softly with party staff about when it would be appropriate to deliver their statement.

Taipei's rain had been falling since afternoon without pause. Watching the rain-soaked broadcast on television, Soong suddenly remarked on a detail: "He should have worn a hat. People coming up from Kaohsiung don't know how cold Taipei's winter rain gets."

There was no irony in his voice. It sounded almost like an uncle noticing a neighbor's nephew going out without enough clothes. Political hatred had never truly taken root inside James Soong. He possessed plenty of grievances and plenty of unfulfilled desires, but hate? Hate required too focused an emotional energy, and he was the kind of man who, at a pre-election rally, would hand his microphone to a rival volunteer who wandered into the wrong venue and ask, "Is the bento over at your place any good?"

At 7:23 PM, the live PFP party-list tally on the Central Election Commission website crossed a critical threshold. Inside the PFP volunteers' chat group, someone sent an entire row of Amitabha Buddha stickers.

Soong didn't look at his phone. He was speaking with Sandra Yu. The two sat on opposite sides of a folding table, upon which sat two bowls of cold pork-ball soup, a thin layer of oil congealed over the surface. Yu had spent thirty years in the advertising industry, reviewing every kind of performance report imaginable, yet she admitted she had never felt her stomach cramp with anxiety over a fraction of a percentage point.

"I once handled a campaign," Yu said. "The client wanted us to sell a dishwashing liquid in thirty seconds, targeting housewives aged forty to fifty-five. The final conversion rate for that ad was four point nine two percent. The client said coming up short by zero point zero eight meant failure. I refused to accept it, arguing that their sample error margin exceeded zero point five. The client replied: 'Ms. Yu, numbers don't care about error margins; numbers are numbers.'"

Soong listened, stirring the oil droplets on the cold soup with a spoon. "Zero point zero six," he said.

Yu was startled: "What?"

"We are short by zero point zero six right now," Soong set down the spoon. "You made that story up, didn't you?"

Yu neither admitted nor denied it. The two sat in silence before the cold soup for a moment. Then Soong stood up, walked to the window, and pushed it open a crack. The scent of winter rain rushed in—wet, cold, carrying the faint metallic rust smell of standing water on asphalt. He stood for a few seconds, closed the window, turned around, his voice lower than before: "But you're right. Numbers don't care about error margins; numbers are numbers."

County-by-county return charts began running across the television screen. Every polling station's tally told the story of this election's outcome. Where were Soong's votes? Scattered across every county and city, like sesame seeds sprinkled over an entire vegetable field—a little bit everywhere, but nowhere enough to gather into a handful. No ballots were issued to him from his birthplace in Hunan on the mainland, nor did his forty years of cultivation in Taipei yield any surprises. His votes were loose items, cast by voters exhausted by both the Kuomintang and the DPP who walked into polling stations on election day, stared at the three sets of names for three seconds, and reached toward the oldest name on the ballot.

A victory without a single county.

Such was James Soong's predicament. Twenty years ago, in the 2000 election, he secured 4.66 million votes, trailing Chen Shui-bian by a mere 300,000. Back then, Chang Chau-hsiung stood beside him, and crowds outside headquarters overflowed onto the boulevard like New Year's Eve. Sixteen years ago, in 2004, he partnered with Lien Chan, and bullets grazed Chen Shui-bian's stomach on the eve of polling in Tainan. Later, in 2012 and 2016, his numbers shrank time after time, shrinking until media networks didn't bother offering him exclusive interviews, shrinking until his debate time allocation felt like a friend's courtesy gift.

Yet today, without taking a single county, he was going to win.

Not a presidential victory. Something stranger, more intimate, closer to a primal instinct. The umbilical cord remained unsevered.

At 8:14 PM, the live Central Election Commission returns updated the final batch of polling stations. Soong's final vote percentage locked onto a number: ${pct.toFixed(1)}%. The ghost of 4.9% was barred at the door. The state subsidies were secured; the entry ticket four years from now was locked in.

When Soong saw the number, he was using a damp wipe to clean his reading glasses. Holding the glasses up to the light to check for smudges, he put them back on, turned toward the TV screen, and paused for two seconds.

In those two seconds, he didn't slam the table, didn't clench his fist, didn't shed a tear, didn't close his eyes and take a deep Hollywood-style breath. He simply let the number sink in quietly, like dropping a copper coin into an iron jar where change had accumulated for forty years—you knew it rang, but you could barely remember why you started saving in the first place.

Sandra Yu walked over and shook his hand. Her hand was dry and firm, knowing how to shake hands better than most male politicians. She said: "Mr. Soong, I walked this journey with you originally just to see what five percent looked like. Now I've seen it."

Soong replied: "It looks a lot like four point nine percent, just a little rounder."

The joke wasn't funny, but everyone in headquarters who heard it laughed anyway. Not because of the joke, but because laughing was the only action they could perform at that moment. You could cry, you could hug, you could call your mother, or you could tell an unfunny joke in front of a room of volunteers and have everyone laugh along with you. That was politics.

Soong walked to the small podium at the front of headquarters. The podium was low, and the microphone stand had to be adjusted three times to align with his mouth. Tapping the microphone with a finger to confirm it was live, he drew a four-folded speech from his inner suit pocket. A tea stain from his afternoon black tea marked the top right corner. Unfolding it flat on the podium, he scanned it once, folded it back up, and placed it back into his pocket.

He began to speak.

"Twenty years ago, in the year 2000, I stood on a much higher podium and told our supporters that we had fought a glorious campaign. Back then, I thought that was the closest I would ever come to the presidency. As it turned out, indeed it was." His voice was steady, without choking, without deliberate slowness. "But that was not my proudest moment. My proudest moment was perhaps on a rainy evening—I can no longer remember which year—at a tiny campaign stop that no news station cared to broadcast, where someone stood under an umbrella listening to every single word I spoke. That person wasn't a local faction boss, wasn't a party worker, just an ordinary citizen who walked over to hear me speak. I still remember the color of that umbrella. Blue, with one broken rib."

Someone in the audience wept. It was a young volunteer, likely participating in a presidential election for the first time, treating late-night phone banking and flyer posting as the most important work of her life. Seeing her tears, Soong offered no comforting cliches, only a subtle nod. He had witnessed too many young passions ground down into gray apathy by political reality throughout his life, refusing to use empty slogans like "Don't cry, we must keep fighting" to address her genuine emotion.

He continued speaking.

"Many people ask me: James Soong, you cannot win, why do you keep running?"

He paused. The question had been roasted on a spit for twenty years across political talk shows, internet forums, and noodle shop chats, and he had offered every version of the answer: for the unfinished work of the provincial governorship, for a pragmatic cross-strait course, for a balancing force inside the system, to keep the People First Party from dissolving. But tonight he spoke none of those.

"I stand here tonight with these old bones simply to tell everyone that Taiwan must never have only two choices, two colors, or two voices. The diverse openness I believed in when entering politics forty years ago cannot be reduced to an empty slogan printed on the back of an election bulletin. We must tell the generations that follow that someone once walked this road first."

As he spoke, the three wrinkles at the corners of his eyes expanded for a brief instant. Those who understood, understood. Years later, on some election night in 2024 or 2028, someone would suddenly recall tonight's unscripted remarks, sit up straight on their couch, and freeze for three seconds.

"So tonight, we did not lose." His voice settled back into its natural low register, like a flat stone skimming the water's surface for a final bounce. "We crossed the umbilical line for this generation. On the road the next generation travels, footprints are already laid."

Stepping down from the podium, his sleeve brushed against a sticky note taped to the edge. The note drifted to the floor, its handwriting becoming clear only as he bent down to pick it up: "Chairman Soong, there is sliced fruit in my home fridge, remember to eat some later."

He didn't know who left it.

He smiled. Not the smile for press cameras, nor the smile for unfunny jokes told to Sandra Yu, but the faint smile that surfaces prior to conscious thought when flipping through a forgotten old photograph from the depths of a drawer in a house you've lived in for forty years.

Rain kept falling outside. Night across Taipei was covered simultaneously by the clamor and silence of election returns. On a few streets, loudspeakers at green headquarters played campaign anthems. In a few alleys, conservative supporters quietly dismantled campaign billboards. Inside PFP headquarters, a 77-year-old man sat back down in that old "Soong-Lin" era chair before the folding table, took a bite of a guava slice someone had prepared, and said to Sandra Yu beside him:

"2024, we won't need signatures anymore."

`; }else { // ------------------------------------------------------------------ // Ending 3: Tsai wins, Soong < 5% (Total Parliamentary Collapse) // ------------------------------------------------------------------ mainTitle = 'Between Five And Four'; subTitle = 'PFP has concluded its career and history in the parliament.'; endImage = 'https://file.garden/Z9XHdyLGHwUEZPS4/ROC%202020/songlose1.png'; s_name = 'Legend - Luo Da-you(1989)'; s_url = 'https://file.garden/Z9XHdyLGHwUEZPS4/ROC%202020/%E5%82%B3%E8%AA%AA%20-%20%E7%BE%85%E5%A4%A7%E4%BD%91%20(youtube).mp3'; s_cover = 'https://file.garden/Z9XHdyLGHwUEZPS4/ROC%202020/chuanshuo.jpg'; endText = `

Four o'clock sharp. The ballots were still dreaming inside their boxes.

James Soong stood by the side door of his campaign headquarters. The sky had turned gray, like a faded poster used one season too many. Hanging inside headquarters was the most respectable banner they had produced in years—after all, his staff had finally learned one skill over the decades: how to spend money where it looked lively. The slogan on the banner was so earnest it felt impolite to ask how many times he had spoken it aloud. He couldn't remember himself. All he knew was that four years ago he had pledged to hand over the torch, eight years ago he had declared it his final run, and twenty years ago crowds had cheered for him to conquer the presidency. Now, standing here, he fought for a single number: five percent.

Not five million votes. Not fifty percent. Just five percent. Clear five, and the party gets subsidies, along with the right to nominate a presidential candidate directly next time—like holding a small brass key to a door. His entire political life had suddenly been compressed into a child getting measured for height: tiptoeing once, tiptoeing twice, still unable to reach the doorbell.

He was seventy-seven years old that year. Other men ran for president to win; he ran to measure his party's height one last time. Campaign aides held out charts for him to inspect: subsidies, party-list votes, legislative seats, the next election's entry ticket—all riding on a single figure. He studied it for a moment and asked, "Are we running for president, or collecting loyalty points at a supermarket?" Nobody laughed. So he stopped smiling, too.

Nobody in headquarters expected him to win. It wasn't even disappointment, just a professional certainty—the way a weather station knows a typhoon is approaching. Staffers set up the press gallery as usual, rehearsed applause as usual, and switched a dozen television sets between two distinct worlds of blue and green. He suddenly found it amusing: at his age, a campaign headquarters felt like a rented banquet hall where even the angle of a bow had been rehearsed in advance.

He ambled over to a folding table against the wall. On it sat a cup of lukewarm tea next to a black-and-white photograph. It was Wan-shui. She had been gone for seven or eight years now, yet whenever night fell, he still felt her in the kitchen, nagging him about wearing too little or taking her teacup by mistake. He still kept that teacup; its base bore a hairline crack from when she accidentally chipped it during his campaign for governor. She used to travel the entire province by his side, shaking hands door to door, smiling until her face froze, refusing to stop. Then she fell ill, terribly ill, yet still told him on live television not to cry. "I won't cry," he had said. She smiled, "You foolish man, even your tears have to prove a point."

Numbers began leaping across the television screens, trickling out in small clusters like beans scattered over a map of Taiwan. Then the blue trend line collapsed while the green soared. On the opposite broadcast, Han Kuo-yu's face sank beneath wave upon wave of incoming returns. Across the sea, the storm that had whipped up over Hong Kong the previous year raged on without pause, rattling the banner of the 1992 Consensus in the gale; compounded by the "runaway mayor" moniker stuck like chewing gum to his shoes through the winter, the man who once stirred a hurricane in Kaohsiung was blown hollow like an empty flagpole. James Soong glanced at it briefly and turned his eyes away. It wasn't out of pity—he had little personal connection with Han. The pan-blue establishment had treated him like a beggar crashing a wedding feast for far too many years. He was accustomed to standing behind the curtain, watching the storm inside the parlor.

When the storm passed, surely it ought to be the turn of the man behind the curtain. The television networks didn't think so. Three major stations, two lead actors, one stage. He and his party were merely the background murmur after the main theme ended—the polite news anchor transition: "As for James Soong..." Returns ticked upward, but his percentage lingered stubbornly at ${pct.toFixed(1)}%, like a cat unable to climb over a doorstep. Five, five, five. He overheard aides whispering in the shadows: "If we don't clear five, those legislative seats..." The sentence remained unfinished, but every shadow under the fluorescent light understood.

Reporters asked him, "Chairman Soong, will you run again after this?" He offered a faint smile and replied, "After this, there is no 'after'." The reporters laughed politely. Even he didn't quite believe the words. He had spoken the line too many times—so many that it had turned into an incantation. Everyone else took it at face value, but he alone knew he was forever waiting for a final destination that never proved to be the end.

His mind drifted to the past. Back then, this road had been wide. During his governorship, he had measured nearly all three hundred-plus townships across Taiwan with his own two feet and crates of provincial highway blueprints. People stood at intersections waiting for him, clutching his hands and shouting "Governor Soong!", as if roads would sprout from the earth at his mere word. He had once believed in something simple: politics should be like a provincial highway, paved for everyone to travel, not gated off for a single family's driveway. He had entered politics with that conviction and walked it for nearly forty years. He had endured the downsizing of the province, broken alliances, been trampled by his own side, and spat on by the other. He had always believed that if he walked long enough, the road itself would prove him right.

Yet on the day Wan-shui died, he suddenly understood: roads weren't meant for proving anything. Roads were meant for walking home. Once she left, more than half the home was gone. He ran in elections afterward anyway, running until her ghost would laugh at him in his dreams: "You foolish man, you even take losing so seriously."

He wondered what it would be like if she were here tonight. She would surely be standing at the entrance of the polling station, fanning herself with a paper fan, telling every passerby, "Our man is here to give everyone an honest campaign." She never expected him to win, but she never asked him to stop running either. She understood that he needed this road, even if it ultimately led nowhere but his own grave.

When the name Lee Teng-hui surfaced in his mind, television screens were broadcasting Tsai Ing-wen's victory speech. Soong thought the old man must be sitting at home, or before a television in a hospital room at Taipei Veterans General, watching this woman secure her second term. Little Tsai, whom Lee had once summoned into the National Security Council to draft cross-strait policy, now sat comfortably in that grand Presidential Office—the very room Soong had yearned for his entire life but never managed to enter. Hearing that the old man's health had failed significantly of late, Soong felt a sudden impulse to place a call to the hospital ward and ask him: "Look at us. We fought each other for a lifetime, carving out a governor's path beyond blue and green. How is it that the road remains, yet not a single traveler is left upon it?" But he didn't dial. On the night of provincial downsizing twenty years ago, the phone line between them had been severed forever. He remembered, too, how the old man used to love saying "Always in my heart, the desires of the people." Many treated the phrase as a political joke nowadays, but Soong knew that in the moment it was spoken, it had been genuine. Just as his own slogan of "provincial governance experience" had been real. It was just that genuine things couldn't endure the relentless sun; exposed too long, they withered into an expired stub, useless for boarding any train.

Blue campaign pamphlets weeks ago were still warning voters that a vote for Soong was a wasted ballot; green talk shows mocked him as a blue satellite, an archaic political specimen. He was pinned between two menus, flipped back and forth like a dessert page nobody ever ordered. Years ago he had been pushed out the door by the Kuomintang; now even the men who pushed him had been swallowed by the whirlpool of history. This was perhaps the most absurd joke of his life: he had expended every ounce of strength to prove Taiwan could walk a road beyond blue and green, yet the moment those two massive party machines ground into gear, even the tiny space that proved his existence was crushed to dust. Abandon one to save the other, abandon one to save the other. The Taiwanese electorate had set him aside in the gentlest possible manner, placing him in the sidebar of the political section accompanied by a small photograph of him waving.

He thought of Wan-shui again. On the day she died, the hospital room was quiet, filled only with the steady drip of the IV tube. He had held her hand; it was already cold. Her last words to him had been, "The road is still long. Walk slowly." He hadn't understood back then. He thought she meant he should walk his political path with more patience, not always rushing to prove his worth. Standing now by the side door, watching his name pinned by returns at ${pct.toFixed(1)}%, he suddenly understood. She hadn't been talking about politics at all—she meant the days left in his life. She was afraid he would walk too fast alone and stumble.

Night deepened. Polling stations extinguished their lights one by one. His final return remained pinned at ${pct.toFixed(1)}% like a pushpin, fastening his name for this final time to an obscure corner of history. He didn't look back. He had long grown accustomed to bidding farewell to numbers.

He took the stage to deliver his concession speech. Press cameras were surprisingly numerous—everyone wanted to see how a man in his late seventies would bring his career to a close. Clearing his throat, he declared that he had lost the numbers, but not the road. His voice was steady, so steady it surprised even himself—as if the words were not coming from his own throat, but streaming from a loudspeaker on some campaign truck from a long-forgotten year, rehearsed so many times they had grown calloused.

Returning to the side door, the hour was late. Tomorrow's headlines across Taiwan were already sealed: Tsai Ing-wen re-elected; Han Kuo-yu defeated. His own news would occupy a small item on the bottom half of the newspaper, or a 0.3-second flash across the television tickertape. He stood there, gazing down the street. The night breeze rattled the flags; streetlight stretched his shadow long, so long it seemed capable of reaching back through all the years he had walked. He realized all at once that what he pursued had never resided within ballot counts. The Presidential Office had never stood at the end of this road. What he sought to prove was simply this: Taiwan could have a road that belonged neither to blue nor to green. Once, that road had been filled with crowds; later, they all picked a side, or were picked for one. Only he remained walking it—a little slower, a little lonelier, like a streetlamp swaying in the wind for far too long.

Reaching into his pocket, he felt the edge of the black-and-white photograph. Wan-shui's smile remained frozen in that summer long ago, wearing a light floral shirt, standing at the main entrance of campaign headquarters, shielding her eyes from the sun with her hand. He suddenly thought that somewhere, she was probably looking down at him with a smile—not laughing at his defeat, but smiling because he had finally agreed to stop, and look back at her properly for once.

He thought to himself, perhaps this was fine too. An old man still walking the road, not for where it led, but simply because the road itself remained alive. He glanced back at campaign headquarters as the lights went out one by one. Turning around, he walked toward home. The soles of his shoes stepped over the threshold, making a faint sound—like that spirited governor from a long-lost year, stepping over the dust of all Taiwan, walking forward one step at a time.

He thought of the past, but there was no one left there. Yet he suddenly felt no fear. A road unwalked did not mean a road erased. As long as someone remembered its contours, remembered the people who once stood beside it, and remembered the woman in the light floral shirt waving to him beneath the sun, the shape of the road remained.

Stepping inside, he switched on the light. Taiwan had survived another noisy election night. And he, at last, could quietly lose the final vote-counting evening of his life. Dawn was near. When the sun rose, he would pour himself a fresh cup of tea, and place it right beside that chipped teacup.

tooltipList.forEach((tooltip, index) => { // Adjust the regex to match searchString potentially surrounded by “ and followed by optional punctuation let regex = new RegExp(`(?<=\\b|\\s|^|“)${tooltip.searchString}(?=[.,;!?]?\\b|\\s|”|$)`, 'g'); let match; while ((match = regex.exec(str)) !== null) { matches.push({ start: match.index + (match[0].startsWith('“') ? 1 : 0), // Adjust for potential starting “ end: match.index + match[0].length - (match[0].endsWith('”') ? 1 : 0) - (match[2] ? 1 : 0), tooltipIndex: index }); } }); // Sort by starting position; if two start at the same position, longer match comes first matches.sort((a, b) => a.start - b.start || b.end - b.start - (a.end - a.start)); // Filter out overlaps for (let i = 0; i < matches.length - 1; ) { if (matches[i + 1].start < matches[i].end) { matches.splice(i + 1, 1); // Remove the next match since it overlaps } else { i++; // Move to next match } } return matches; } function applyTooltips(str) { const matches = getTooltips(str); let result = ''; let lastIndex = 0; // 获取当前候选人 ID const currentCandidatePK = campaignTrail_temp.candidate_id; matches.forEach(match => { const tooltip = tooltipList[match.tooltipIndex]; result += str.slice(lastIndex, match.start); // --- 核心逻辑：根据 PK 选取定制文本 --- let finalExplanationText = tooltip.explanationText; try { const customData = JSON.parse(tooltip.explanationText); if (currentCandidatePK && customData[currentCandidatePK]) { finalExplanationText = customData[currentCandidatePK]; } else if (customData.default) { finalExplanationText = customData.default; } } catch (e) { // 如果解析失败，说明它是非定制的普通字符串，保持不变 finalExplanationText = tooltip.explanationText; } // ------------------------------------ result += `${tooltip.searchString}${finalExplanationText}`; lastIndex = match.end; }); result += str.slice(lastIndex); // Add the remainder of the original string return result; } function applyTooltipsToObject(obj) { for (let key in obj) { if (typeof obj[key] === 'string') { obj[key] = applyTooltips(obj[key]); } else if (typeof obj[key] === 'object' && obj[key] !== null) { applyTooltipsToObject(obj[key]); // Recursive call } } } // 只有当玩家在 Code 1 选择了开启（默认）时，才运行注释替换 if (campaignTrail_temp.tooltip_enabled) { applyTooltipsToObject(campaignTrail_temp.questions_json); Data Processing Helper Functions // ================================================================= function hijackPVSwitcher() { const pvButton = document.querySelector('#pvswitcher'); if (pvButton) { if (pvButton.textContent.trim() === '') return; pvButton.removeAttribute('onclick'); pvButton.textContent = ''; pvButton.style.textDecoration = 'none'; } } function checkCampaignTrailData() { return (typeof campaignTrail_temp !== 'undefined' && campaignTrail_temp !== null && campaignTrail_temp.current_results && campaignTrail_temp.current_results[0]); } function getRandomAdjustment(min, max) { return Math.random() * (max - min) + min; } if (typeof PollAdjustmentCache === 'undefined') { var PollAdjustmentCache = {}; } function generateModData() { if (!checkCampaignTrailData()) { CurrentPollResults = []; return; } let original_results = JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(campaignTrail_temp.current_results[0])); CurrentPollResults = []; original_results.sort((a, b) => b.pvp - a.pvp); const currentQuestionNumber = typeof e !== 'undefined' && e && typeof e.question_number === 'number' ? e.question_number : Object.keys(PollAdjustmentCache).length; const displayRound = currentQuestionNumber + 1; const cacheKey = `R${displayRound}`; let totalAdjustedPVP = 0; const adjustedResultsMap = new Map(); original_results.forEach((candidate) => { let pvp = candidate.pvp; let adjustment = 0; if (PollAdjustmentCache[candidate.pk] !== undefined && typeof PollAdjustmentCache[candidate.pk] === 'number') { adjustment = PollAdjustmentCache[candidate.pk]; } else { switch (candidate.pk) { case DPP_PK: adjustment = getRandomAdjustment(-0.02, 0.03); break; case KMT_PK: adjustment = getRandomAdjustment(-0.04, 0.02); break; case SOONG_PK: adjustment = getRandomAdjustment(-0.02, 0.03); break; default: adjustment = 0; } PollAdjustmentCache[candidate.pk] = adjustment; } let adjustedPVP = pvp + adjustment; if (adjustedPVP < 0) adjustedPVP = 0; totalAdjustedPVP += adjustedPVP; adjustedResultsMap.set(candidate.pk, adjustedPVP); }); if (totalAdjustedPVP <= 0) totalAdjustedPVP = 1; original_results.forEach((candidate) => { let adjustedPVP = adjustedResultsMap.get(candidate.pk) || 0; let normalizedPVP = adjustedPVP / totalAdjustedPVP; const voteShare = (normalizedPVP * 100).toFixed(1); let color = '#808080', party = 'N/A'; // 1. 只映射政党和颜色 switch (candidate.pk) { case DPP_PK: color = '#1b9431'; party = 'DPP'; break; case KMT_PK: color = '#000099'; party = 'KMT'; break; case SOONG_PK: color = '#FF6600'; party = 'PFP'; break; } // 2. 动态去 CANDIDATE_DATA 里抓取名字，彻底告别硬编码 const candidateInfo = CANDIDATE_DATA.find(c => c.party === party); const dynamicName = candidateInfo ? campaignTrail_temp.candidate_id : KMT_PK); for (const item of rawData) { if (item.fields && item.fields.candidate === currentId) { scores[item.fields.issue] = item.fields.issue_score; } } [32, 33, 34, 35, 36].forEach(issueId => { if (scores[issueId] === undefined) scores[issueId] = 0.0; }); return scores; } const policyScores = getPolicyScores(); let tableRows = ''; [32, 33, 34, 35, 36].forEach(issueId => { const score = policyScores[issueId]; const issue = POLICY_ISSUES[issueId]; // 1. 针对 Youth Appeal (Issue 36) 进行逻辑反转！ // 如果是 36 题，分数越负反而越蓝，所以我们把它的分数乘以 -1 再去判定颜色 let effectiveScore = (issueId == 36) ? `; } // === 纯净新增：立法院选项卡 (含 2016 国会席次可视化与动态变量支持) === function createLegislativeContent(container, e) { const context = e || {}; // --- 动态抓取变量，如果没有则回退到 2016 年的历史真实席次 --- const seatsDPP = context.SeatsDPP || 68; // 民进党 const seatsKMT = context.SeatsKMT || 35; // 国民党 const seatsNPP = context.SeatsNPP || 5; // 时代力量 (New Power Party) const seatsPFP = context.SeatsPFP || 3; // 亲民党 const seatsIND = context.SeatsIND || 2; // 无党团结联盟1 + 无党籍1 const totalSeats = 113; const majorityThreshold = 57; // 过半席次 // --- 颜色配置 (泛绿 -> 泛蓝) --- const colorDPP = '#1b9431'; const colorNPP = '#F9BE01'; // 时代力量黄 const colorIND = '#808080'; const colorPFP = '#FF6600'; const colorKMT = '#000099'; // --- 立法院长与头像预留位 --- const speakerName = context.LYSpeakerName || 'Su Jia-chyuan (DPP)'; // 在下面这行替换你想要的院长头像URL const speakerAvatar = context.LYSpeakerAvatar || 'https://file.garden/Z9XHdyLGHwUEZPS4/ROC%202020/500px-Su_Jia-chyuan_in_2016_LY_altered.png'; // --- 计算宽度百分比 --- const pctDPP = (seatsDPP / totalSeats) * 100; const pctNPP = (seatsNPP / totalSeats) * 100; const pctIND = (seatsIND / totalSeats) * 100; const pctPFP = (seatsPFP / totalSeats) * 100; const pctKMT = (seatsKMT / totalSeats) * 100; // --- 多数党状态判定 --- let majorityStatus = 'Hung Parliament (悬峙国会)'; let majorityColor = '#808080'; if (seatsDPP >= majorityThreshold) { majorityStatus = 'DPP Absolute Majority'; majorityColor = colorDPP; } else if (seatsKMT >= majorityThreshold) { majorityStatus = 'KMT Absolute Majority'; majorityColor = colorKMT; } container.style.textAlign = 'left'; container.innerHTML = `

Final Election Result Chart Integration (Modernized) // ================================================================= function getFinalResultsAsTrendData(nextTurnIndex) { if (!campaignTrail_temp || !campaignTrail_temp.candidate_json) return []; const getPvpByPk = (pk) => { const candidate = campaignTrail_temp.candidate_json.find(c => c.pk === pk); return candidate ? candidate.pvp * 100 : 0; }; let final_chen_pvp = getPvpByPk(DPP_PK); let final_lien_pvp = getPvpByPk(KMT_PK); let final_soong_pvp = getPvpByPk(SOONG_PK); let finalData = []; // 动态获取名字并加上 (Actual) 后缀 const getDynamicName = (partyName) => { const cInfo = CANDIDATE_DATA.find(c => c.party === partyName); return cInfo ? cInfo.name + ' (Actual)' : partyName + ' (Actual)'; }; if (final_chen_pvp > 0) { finalData.push({ turn: nextTurnIndex, pk: DPP_PK, name: getDynamicName('DPP'), vote: final_chen_pvp, color: '#1b9431' }); } if (final_lien_pvp > 0) { finalData.push({ turn: nextTurnIndex, pk: KMT_PK, name: getDynamicName('KMT'), vote: final_lien_pvp, color: '#000099' }); } if (final_soong_pvp > 0) { finalData.push({ turn: nextTurnIndex, pk: SOONG_PK, name: getDynamicName('PFP'), vote: final_soong_pvp, color: '#FF6600' }); } return finalData; } function openFinalChartWindow() { let historicalData = compileHistoricalPollData(); let maxTurn = historicalData.length > 0 ? Observer Logic (Initialization & Endgame) // ================================================================= let observerRunning = false; function handleMutations(mutationsList, observer) { if (observerRunning) return; observerRunning = true; if (typeof generateModData === 'function') generateModData(); if (typeof addDataCenterButton === 'function') addDataCenterButton(); observer.observe(document.documentElement, { childList: true, subtree: true }); observerRunning = false; } let element = document.getElementById('controlElement'); if (!element) { let singleObserver = new MutationObserver(handleMutations); singleObserver.observe(document.documentElement, { childList: true, subtree: true }); let controlElement = document.createElement('div'); controlElement.style.display = 'none'; controlElement.id = 'controlElement'; document.body.appendChild(controlElement); } const finalScreenObserver = new MutationObserver(function(mutations) { checkForFinalScreen(); }); finalScreenObserver.observe(document.body, { childList: true, subtree: true }); checkForFinalScreen(); // 简单粗暴，直接强行同步 // 使用一个固定的变量名，防止重复声明报错 if (window.imageInterval) clearInterval(window.imageInterval); window.imageInterval = setInterval(() => { try { let candImg = document.querySelector(".person_image"); let desc = document.getElementById("final_results_description"); // 1. 如果元素还没加载出来，直接跳过，等待下一帧，防止报错 if (!candImg) return; // 2. 核心逻辑：根据 e.page 判断 let LY_IMAGE = 'https://file.garden/Z9XHdyLGHwUEZPS4/ROC%202020/800x600_wmkn_443324537264_0.jpg?v=1782587658664'; // 如果是第2页（Page 1），强制显示立法院图 if (e && e.page === 1) { if (candImg.src !== LY_IMAGE) { candImg.src = LY_IMAGE; } } // 否则（Page 0 或其他），使用系统原本的逻辑 else { if (e.image === true) { candImg.style.display = 'none'; // 隐藏图片，避免显示错误 } else if (e.image && candImg.src !== e.image) { candImg.src = e.image; candImg.style.display = 'block'; } } // 3. 样式修正 if (desc) { desc.style.display = 'block'; desc.style.marginLeft = 'auto'; } } catch (err) { // 出现任何意外，直接吞掉报错，不影响游戏运行 } }, 200); // 200毫秒轮询一次，体感上就是“秒执行”
